\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.1}
\begin{statement}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be two Hilbert spaces, $K:X\mapsto Y$ a linear and
continuous operator with adjoint $K^*:Y\mapsto X$, $f\in Y$, and the least
squares functional $F:X\mapsto\R$ given by $F(v)=\norm{Kv-f}_Y^2$, for all
$v\in X$. Show that $u\in X$ is a minimizer of $F$ if and only if
$K^*(Ku)=K^*f$.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
For $h\in X$ and $t\in\R$,
\begin{align*}
 F(u+th)-F(u)
 &=2t\operatorname{Re}\ip{K^*(Ku-f)}{h}_X+t^2\norm{Kh}_Y^2
\end{align*}
At a minimizer, differentiation at $t=0$ with $h=K^*(Ku-f)$ gives
$K^*Ku=K^*f$. Conversely,
\begin{align*}
 F(u+h)-F(u)&=\norm{Kh}_Y^2\geq0
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.2}
\begin{statement}
Let $X$ and $Y$ be two Hilbert spaces, $K:X\mapsto Y$ a linear and
continuous operator, $f\in Y$, and $\lambda>0$. Show that the minimization
problem
\begin{align*}
 \inf_{v\in X}\left\{\norm{Kv-f}_Y^2+\lambda\norm{v}_X^2\right\}
\end{align*}
has a unique solution $u\in X$, given by
$u=(K^*K+\lambda I)^{-1}(K^*f)$, where $I$ is the identity operator on $X$,
and $K^*:Y\mapsto X$ is the adjoint of $K$.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
Let $F$ denote the objective and set $A=K^*K+\lambda I$. Since
$A=A^*$,
\begin{align*}
 \ip{Av}{v}_X&=\norm{Kv}_Y^2+\lambda\norm{v}_X^2
 \geq\lambda\norm{v}_X^2\\
 \norm{Av}_X&\geq\lambda\norm{v}_X
\end{align*}
Thus $A$ is injective with closed range. Since
$(\operatorname{ran}A)^\perp=\ker A^*=\ker A=\{0\}$, it is onto.
For $u=A^{-1}K^*f$ and $h\ne0$,
\begin{align*}
 F(u+h)-F(u)
 &=2\operatorname{Re}\ip{Au-K^*f}{h}_X
   +\norm{Kh}_Y^2+\lambda\norm{h}_X^2\\
 &=\norm{Kh}_Y^2+\lambda\norm{h}_X^2>0
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.3}
\begin{statement}
Let $X$, $Y$ and $Z$ be Hilbert spaces, $K:X\mapsto Y$ a linear and
continuous operator with adjoint $K^*:Y\mapsto X$, $L:X\mapsto Z$ another
linear and continuous operator with adjoint $L^*:Z\mapsto X$, $f\in Y$,
and $\lambda>0$. Show that $u\in X$ is solution of the minimization problem
\begin{align*}
 \inf_{v\in X}\left\{\norm{Kv-f}_Y^2+\lambda\norm{Lv}_Z^2\right\}
\end{align*}
if and only if $u$ satisfies
\begin{align*}
 (K^*K+\lambda L^*L)u=(K^*f)
\end{align*}
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
Let $F$ denote the objective and put
$g=(K^*K+\lambda L^*L)u-K^*f$. For $h\in X$ and $t\in\R$,
\begin{align*}
 F(u+th)-F(u)
 &=2t\operatorname{Re}\ip{g}{h}_X
   +t^2\bigl(\norm{Kh}_Y^2+\lambda\norm{Lh}_Z^2\bigr)
\end{align*}
At a minimizer, differentiation at $t=0$ with $h=g$ gives $g=0$.
Conversely, if $g=0$, then
\begin{align*}
 F(u+h)-F(u)&=\norm{Kh}_Y^2+\lambda\norm{Lh}_Z^2\geq0
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.4}
\begin{statement}
Prove Proposition 1.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
All integrals are over $\R^N$. For $k,u,v\in L^1$, Fubini applies
almost everywhere since the absolute triple integral is
$\norm{k}_1\norm{u}_1\norm{v}_1$.

\partlabel{i}
\begin{align*}
 (k*u)(x)&=\int k(x-y)u(y)\,dy\\
 &=\int k(t)u(x-t)\,dt=(u*k)(x)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{ii} By Fubini and $s=y+t$,
\begin{align*}
 ((k*u)*v)(x)
 &=\iint k(x-y-t)u(t)v(y)\,dt\,dy\\
 &=\int k(x-s)\left(\int u(t)v(s-t)\,dt\right)ds\\
 &=(k*(u*v))(x)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{iii} With $\tau_zu(x)=u(x-z)$,
\begin{align*}
 ((\tau_zk)*u)(x)
 &=\int k(x-z-y)u(y)\,dy=\tau_z(k*u)(x)\\
 (k*(\tau_zu))(x)
 &=\int k(x-y)u(y-z)\,dy\\
 &=\int k(x-z-t)u(t)\,dt=\tau_z(k*u)(x)
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.5}
\begin{statement}
Prove Young's inequality from Proposition 2.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
Let $k\in L^1(\R^N)$ and $u\in L^p(\R^N)$. If $M=\norm{k}_1>0$,
set $d\mu(y)=|k(y)|\,dy/M$. For $1\leq p<\infty$, H\"older gives
\begin{align*}
 \left(\int|k(y)u(x-y)|\,dy\right)^p
 &\leq M^p\int|u(x-y)|^p\,d\mu(y)
\end{align*}
By Tonelli, $k*u$ exists almost everywhere and
$\norm{k*u}_p\leq M\norm{u}_p$. The case $M=0$ is immediate.
For $p=\infty$,
\begin{align*}
 |(k*u)(x)|&\leq\int|k(y)u(x-y)|\,dy
 \leq\norm{k}_1\norm{u}_\infty
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.6}
\begin{statement}
Prove Theorem 1.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
Use $\widehat u(\xi)=\int u(x)e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot x}\dx$ and
$u,v\in L^1(\R^N)$.

\partlabel{i} For $\tau_yu(x)=u(x-y)$,
\begin{align*}
 \widehat{\tau_yu}(\xi)
 &=\int u(t)e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot(t+y)}\,dt\\
 &=e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot y}\widehat u(\xi)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{ii} By Young's inequality and Fubini,
\begin{align*}
 \widehat{u*v}(\xi)
 &=\iint u(x-y)v(y)e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot x}\,dy\dx\\
 &=\iint u(t)v(y)e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot(t+y)}\,dt\,dy\\
 &=\widehat u(\xi)\widehat v(\xi)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{iii} Suppose $x^\alpha u\in L^1$ for $|\alpha|\leq k$.
Dominated convergence gives, for $|\alpha|\leq k$,
\begin{align*}
 \partial^\alpha\widehat u(\xi)
 &=\int(-2\pi ix)^\alpha u(x)e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot x}\dx\\
 &=\widehat{(-2\pi ix)^\alpha u}(\xi)
\end{align*}
Dominated convergence also gives $\widehat u\in C^k$.

\partlabel{iv} Suppose $u\in C^k$ and $\partial^\alpha u\in L^1$ for
$|\alpha|\leq k$. Choose $\chi\in C_c^\infty$ equal to one near zero
and put $\chi_R(x)=\chi(x/R)$. Integration by parts gives
\begin{align*}
 \int\chi_R\partial_j u\,e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot x}\dx
 &=2\pi i\xi_j\int\chi_Ru\,e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot x}\dx\\
 &\quad-\int(\partial_j\chi_R)u\,e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot x}\dx
\end{align*}
The last term is bounded by
$R^{-1}\norm{\partial_j\chi}_\infty\norm{u}_1\to0$.
Letting $R\to\infty$ and iterating gives
\begin{align*}
 \widehat{\partial^\alpha u}(\xi)&=(2\pi i\xi)^\alpha\widehat u(\xi)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{v} By Fubini,
\begin{align*}
 \int\widehat u(\xi)v(\xi)\,d\xi
 &=\iint u(x)v(\xi)e^{-2\pi i\xi\cdot x}\dx\,d\xi\\
 &=\int u(x)\widehat v(x)\dx
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.7}
\begin{statement}
Let $f\in L^1$ be a continuous function such that $\widehat f\in L^1$.
Prove that $(\widehat f)^\vee=(\check f)^\wedge=f$.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
For $g(s)=e^{-\pi s^2}$, the identities of Exercise 2.6 give
\begin{align*}
 (\widehat g)'(\xi)&=-2\pi\xi\widehat g(\xi)\\
 \widehat g(0)&=1\\
 \widehat g(\xi)&=e^{-\pi\xi^2}
\end{align*}
Taking products and rescaling gives, for $t>0$,
\begin{align*}
 G_t(x)&=t^{-N/2}e^{-\pi|x|^2/t}\\
 \int_{\R^N}G_t(x)\,dx&=1\\
 \int_{\R^N}e^{-\pi t|\xi|^2}e^{2\pi i x\cdot\xi}\,d\xi&=G_t(x)
\end{align*}
Since $f\in L^1$, Fubini gives
\begin{align*}
 I_t(x)
 &:=\int_{\R^N}\widehat f(\xi)e^{-\pi t|\xi|^2}
                 e^{2\pi i x\cdot\xi}\,d\xi\\
 &=\int_{\R^N}f(y)G_t(x-y)\,dy
\end{align*}
As $\widehat f\in L^1$, dominated convergence yields
\begin{align*}
 I_t(x)&\longrightarrow(\widehat f)^\vee(x)
\end{align*}
Fix $x$ and $\eta>0$. Choose $\delta>0$ so that
$|f(x-z)-f(x)|<\eta$ for $|z|<\delta$. Then
\begin{align*}
 |I_t(x)-f(x)|
 &\leq\eta+t^{-N/2}e^{-\pi\delta^2/t}\norm f_1
       +|f(x)|\int_{|z|\geq\delta}G_t(z)\,dz
\end{align*}
Letting $t\downarrow0$, then $\eta\downarrow0$, gives
$(\widehat f)^\vee(x)=f(x)$. Since $\check f(\xi)=\widehat f(-\xi)$,
\begin{align*}
 (\check f)^\wedge(x)
 &=\int_{\R^N}\widehat f(-\xi)e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}\,d\xi\\
 &=\int_{\R^N}\widehat f(\xi)e^{2\pi i x\cdot\xi}\,d\xi\\
 &=f(x)
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}


\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.8}
\begin{statement}
Take $\Omega=(-1,1)$ and $u(x)=|x|$.
\par\smallskip
(a) Compute the (weak) derivative $v$ of $u$ and show that
$u\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)$.
\par\smallskip
(b) Show that $v'$ does not exist in the weak sense.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
\partlabel{a} Set $v=\operatorname{sgn}$. For $\varphi\in C_c^\infty(-1,1)$,
\begin{align*}
 -\int_{-1}^1|x|\varphi'(x)\,dx
 &=\int_{-1}^1v(x)\varphi(x)\,dx\\
 \norm{u}_\infty&=\norm{v}_\infty=1
\end{align*}

\partlabel{b} Distributionally,
\begin{align*}
 \langle Dv,\varphi\rangle
 &=-\int_{-1}^1\operatorname{sgn}(x)\varphi'(x)\,dx
 =2\varphi(0)
\end{align*}
Hence $Dv=2\delta_0$. If it had a density $g\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(-1,1)$,
choose $\eta\in C_c^\infty(-1,1)$ with $0\le\eta\le1$ and $\eta(0)=1$.
Testing with $\eta(x/\eps)$ would give
\begin{align*}
 2&=\left|\int_{-1}^1g(x)\eta(x/\eps)\,dx\right|\\
 &\le\int_{-\eps}^{\eps}|g(x)|\,dx\longrightarrow0
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.9}
\begin{statement}
Show existence and uniqueness of minimizers of the problem
\begin{align*}
 \inf_{u\in H^1(\Omega)}
 \left\{F(u)=\int_\Omega|\nabla u|^2\dx
                  +\lambda\int_\Omega(u-f)^2\dx\right\}
\end{align*}
where $\Omega$ is an open, bounded and connected subset of $\R^2$
with Lipschitz boundary $\partial\Omega$, $f\in L^2(\Omega)$ is a
real-valued image function, and $\lambda>0$ is a given parameter.
Also, compute the associated Euler--Lagrange equation of the functional $F$.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
On $H^1(\Omega)$, set
\begin{align*}
 a(v,h)&=\int_\Omega\nabla v\cdot\nabla h\dx
                         +\lambda\int_\Omega vh\dx\\
 \ell(h)&=\lambda\int_\Omega fh\dx
\end{align*}
Both are continuous, and
\begin{align*}
 a(v,v)&\ge\min\{1,\lambda\}\norm v_{H^1}^2
\end{align*}
Lax--Milgram gives a unique $u\in H^1(\Omega)$ with $a(u,h)=\ell(h)$
for every $h\in H^1(\Omega)$. For $h\ne0$,
\begin{align*}
 F(u+h)-F(u)&=2a(u,h)-2\ell(h)+a(h,h)\\
 &=\norm{\nabla h}_2^2+\lambda\norm h_2^2>0
\end{align*}
The Euler--Lagrange equation is $-\Delta u+\lambda u=\lambda f$ in
$\Omega$, with $\partial_nu=0$ on $\partial\Omega$, understood weakly.
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.10}
\begin{statement}
Let $u:\mathbb R^2\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb R$ be a smooth solution of
\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}=|Du|G(curv(u))
\end{align*}
where $curv(u)=div\left(\frac{Du}{|Du|}\right)$ is the curvature operator in two spatial dimensions, $t\ge0$, and $G$ is a continuous and nondecreasing function such that $kG(k)\ge0$. Show that the total sum of the perimeters of level lines decreases in time.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
Assume regular level curves, finite integrated perimeter, differentiation
under the level integral, and vanishing boundary terms. Write $n=\nabla u/|\nabla u|$ and $\kappa=\diver n$.
For $u(C(t),t)=a$ and $V=C_t\cdot n$,
\begin{align*}
 0&=u_t+\nabla u\cdot C_t\\
 V&=-G(\kappa)
\end{align*}
For the unit tangent $T$ and arclength $s$, $T_s=-\kappa n$.
Integration by parts gives
\begin{align*}
 L_a'(t)&=-\int_{\{u=a\}}T_s\cdot C_t\,ds\\
 &=\int_{\{u=a\}}\kappa V\,ds\\
 &=-\int_{\{u=a\}}\kappa G(\kappa)\,ds\le0
\end{align*}
By coarea,
\begin{align*}
 P(t)&=\int_\R L_a(t)\,da
       =\int_{\R^2}|\nabla u(x,t)|\,dx\\
 P'(t)&=-\int_\R\int_{\{u=a\}}\kappa G(\kappa)\,ds\,da\le0
\end{align*}
\finish

\newpage
\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.11}
\begin{statement}
Let $u:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R$ be a function in $C^2(\mathbb R^2)$. Let $x\in\mathbb R^2$ be such that $|Du|(x)\ne0$.
\par\smallskip
(a) Verify the formula
\begin{align*}
\Delta u=u_{\vec N\vec N}+u_{\vec T\vec T}
\end{align*}
where $\vec N$ and $\vec T$ are two unit orthogonal vectors at $x$, such that $\vec N=\frac{Du(x)}{|Du|(x)}$ is normal to the level line of $u$ passing through $x$ (as in Figure 1.3), and $u_{\vec N\vec N}=\vec N^tD^2u\vec N$ and $u_{\vec T\vec T}=\vec T^tD^2u\vec T$ are the second order derivatives of $u$ at $x$ in the $\vec N$ and $\vec T$ directions, respectively.
\par\smallskip
(b) Verify the formula at $x$:
\begin{align*}
div\left(\frac{Du}{|Du|}\right)=\frac1{|Du|}u_{\vec T\vec T}
\end{align*}
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
\partlabel{a} At $x$, write $H=D^2u$, $N=\vec N$, and $T=\vec T$.
Since $NN^t+TT^t=I$,
\begin{align*}
 \Delta u&=\operatorname{tr}H\\
 &=\operatorname{tr}(HNN^t)+\operatorname{tr}(HTT^t)\\
 &=N^tHN+T^tHT=u_{NN}+u_{TT}
\end{align*}

\partlabel{b} For $s=|\nabla u|>0$, $\nabla s=H\nabla u/s$, so
\begin{align*}
 \diver\left(\frac{\nabla u}{s}\right)
 &=\frac{\Delta u}{s}-\frac{\nabla u\cdot\nabla s}{s^2}\\
 &=\frac{\Delta u-N^tHN}{s}\\
 &=\frac{u_{TT}}{|\nabla u|}
\end{align*}
\finish


\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.12}
\begin{statement}
Using the notations from Section 2.8, show that the length functional is invariant of the parametrization.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
For a regular $C^1$ curve $C:[a,b]\to\R^2$ and a $C^1$
diffeomorphism $r:[\alpha,\beta]\to[a,b]$, set $\widetilde C=C\circ r$.
By substitution,
\begin{align*}
 L(\widetilde C)
 &=\int_\alpha^\beta|C'(r(q))|\,|r'(q)|\,dq\\
 &=\int_a^b|C'(s)|\,ds=L(C)
\end{align*}
\finish

\newpage
\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.13}
\begin{statement}
Consider the one-dimensional length minimization problem
\[
\min_u\left\{F(u)=\int_0^1\sqrt{1+(u'(x))^2}\,dx\right\}
\]
over functions $u:[0,1]\to\mathbb R$ in $C^1([0,1])$ with boundary conditions $u(0)=0$ and $u(1)=1$.
\par\smallskip
(a) Assuming that a minimizer $u\in C^2([0,1])$, compute the associated Euler--Lagrange equation and find the exact solution of the minimization.
\par\smallskip
(b) Show that the functional $u\mapsto F(u)$ over a convex set, is convex.
\par\smallskip
(c) Consider a discrete version of the problem: let
\[
x_0=0<x_1<\cdots<x_n<x_{n+1}=1
\]
be equidistant points, with $x_{i+1}-x_i=h$. For $\vec u=(u_1,\ldots,u_n)$, consider
\[
f(\vec u)=h\sum_{i=0}^n\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{u_{i+1}-u_i}{h}\right)^2}
\]
with the additional condition that $u_0=0$ and $u_{n+1}=1$. Choose an appropriate discretization integer $n$. Then numerically and experimentally analyze the behavior of the gradient descent method applied to $f$. Choose the initial starting point $u^0$ as a curve joining the points $(0,0)$ and $(1,1)$. Record the number of iterations $k$ and plot the error $u^k-u^*$, where $u^*$ is the exact minimizer.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
\partlabel{a}
Set $g(s)=\sqrt{1+s^2}$. For a variation $v$ vanishing at the endpoints,
\begin{align*}
 DF(u)[v]
 &=\int_0^1\frac{u'v'}{\sqrt{1+(u')^2}}\,dx\\
 &=-\int_0^1\left(\frac{u'}{\sqrt{1+(u')^2}}\right)'v\,dx
\end{align*}
Thus
\begin{align*}
 \left(\frac{u'}{\sqrt{1+(u')^2}}\right)'
 &=\frac{u''}{(1+(u')^2)^{3/2}}=0
\end{align*}
The endpoint conditions give $u^*(x)=x$.
Since $g''(s)=(1+s^2)^{-3/2}>0$,
\begin{align*}
 g(s)&\ge\frac{1+s}{\sqrt2}\\
 F(u)&\ge\frac1{\sqrt2}\int_0^1(1+u')\,dx=\sqrt2=F(u^*)
\end{align*}

\newpage
\partlabel{b}
For $0<t<1$, convexity of $g$ gives
\begin{align*}
 F(tu+(1-t)v)
 &=\int_0^1g(tu'+(1-t)v')\,dx\\
 &\le tF(u)+(1-t)F(v)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{c}
Put $s_i=(u_{i+1}-u_i)/h$ and $q(s)=g'(s)$.
With endpoints fixed,
\begin{align*}
 (\nabla f(u))_j&=q(s_{j-1})-q(s_j)\\
 u_j^{k+1}&=u_j^k-\tau\bigl(q(s_{j-1}^k)-q(s_j^k)\bigr)
\end{align*}
For $v_0=v_{n+1}=0$,
\begin{align*}
 v^tD^2f(u)v
 &=\frac1h\sum_{i=0}^ng''(s_i)(v_{i+1}-v_i)^2\\
 &\le\frac4h\norm v_2^2
\end{align*}
Taylor's inequality gives
\begin{align*}
 f(u-\tau\nabla f(u))
 &\le f(u)-\tau\left(1-\frac{2\tau}{h}\right)\norm{\nabla f(u)}_2^2
\end{align*}
Choose $0<\tau<h/2$. Since
\begin{align*}
 f(u)&\ge\frac h{\sqrt2}\sum_{i=0}^n(1+s_i)=\sqrt2
\end{align*}
with equality only when $s_i=1$, the minimizer is $u_j^*=x_j$.

Use $n=31$, $h=1/32$, $\tau=0.45h=0.0140625$, and
\begin{align*}
 u_j^0&=x_j+0.2\sin(\pi x_j)+0.05\sin(3\pi x_j)
\end{align*}
The stopping criterion $\norm{\nabla f(u^k)}_\infty\le10^{-9}$
first held at $k=11028$.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabular}{r r r r}
$k$ & $f(u^k)$ & $\|u^k-u^*\|_\infty$ & $\|\nabla f(u^k)\|_\infty$\\
\hline
0 & 1.475737665480 & $1.782\cdot10^{-1}$ & $1.382\cdot10^{-1}$ \\
100 & 1.440282005694 & $1.589\cdot10^{-1}$ & $2.328\cdot10^{-2}$ \\
1000 & 1.415871086434 & $4.359\cdot10^{-2}$ & $4.754\cdot10^{-3}$ \\
5000 & 1.414213570192 & $9.471\cdot10^{-5}$ & $1.032\cdot10^{-5}$ \\
11028 & 1.414213562373 & $9.164\cdot10^{-9}$ & $9.985\cdot10^{-10}$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=7cm,y=18cm,font=\small]
\draw[->] (0,0)--(1.06,0) node[right] {$x$};
\draw[->] (0,0)--(0,.245) node[above] {$u^k-u^*$};

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{2.14}
\begin{statement}
Using Taylor's formula, show that each derivative in (2.15), (2.16), (2.17), and (2.18) is approximated by the given formula with the corresponding error.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
Set $h=\Delta x$ and $x=x_j$. All Taylor intervals lie in the domain.

For $u\in C^2$, Taylor's theorem gives
\begin{align*}
 u(x\pm h)&=u(x)\pm hu'(x)+\frac{h^2}{2}u''(\xi_\pm)
\end{align*}
where $\xi_+\in(x,x+h)$ and $\xi_-\in(x-h,x)$. Hence
\begin{align*}
 u'(x)-\frac{u(x+h)-u(x)}h&=-\frac h2u''(\xi_+)\\
 u'(x)-\frac{u(x)-u(x-h)}h&=\frac h2u''(\xi_-)
\end{align*}
Both absolute errors are at most $h\norm{u''}_\infty/2$.

For $u\in C^3$, subtracting the second-order Taylor expansions gives
\begin{align*}
 u'(x)-\frac{u(x+h)-u(x-h)}{2h}
 &=-\frac{h^2}{12}\bigl(u'''(\eta_+)+u'''(\eta_-)\bigr)\\
 &=-\frac{h^2}{6}u'''(\xi)
\end{align*}
Here $\eta_\pm$ lie in the respective one-sided intervals and
$\xi\in(x-h,x+h)$. The absolute error is at most
$h^2\norm{u'''}_\infty/6$.

For $u\in C^4$, adding the third-order Taylor expansions gives,
with $\eta_\pm,\zeta\in(x-h,x+h)$,
\begin{align*}
 u''(x)-\frac{u(x+h)-2u(x)+u(x-h)}{h^2}
 &=-\frac{h^2}{24}\bigl(u^{(4)}(\eta_+)+u^{(4)}(\eta_-)\bigr)\\
 &=-\frac{h^2}{12}u^{(4)}(\zeta)
\end{align*}
\finish

\pdfbookmark[0]{Chapter 3}{chapter3}
\begin{center}
\textbf{Chapter 3}
\end{center}

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.1}
\begin{statement}
Consider the general image restoration functional
\begin{align*}
 F(u)&=\int_\Omega\phi(|Du|)+\lambda\int_\Omega(f-Ku)^2\,dx
 \tag{3.18}
\end{align*}
with $f,u:\Omega\to\mathbb R$, $\Omega$ an open and bounded subset
of $\mathbb R^2$, $f\in L^2(\Omega)$,
$K:L^2(\Omega)\to L^2(\Omega)$ a linear and continuous operator,
and $\phi:[0,\infty)\mapsto[0,\infty)$ continuous and increasing,
with $\phi(|t|)\le a|t|+b$ for all $t$, $a,b$ constants and $a>0$.
If $\phi$ is convex, show that the functional $F$ is convex on
$BV(\Omega)$. When is $F$ strictly convex?
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
Take $\lambda>0$ and $X=\BV(\Omega)\cap L^2(\Omega)$.
For bounded Lipschitz $\Omega\subset\R^2$, $X=\BV(\Omega)$.
Write $Du=\nabla u\,dx+D^su$ and set
\begin{align*}
 \ell&=\lim_{s\to\infty}\frac{\phi(s)}s\in[0,a]\\
 J(u)&=\int_\Omega\phi(|\nabla u|)\,dx+\ell|D^su|(\Omega)
\end{align*}
For $0<t<1$, monotonicity and convexity give
\begin{align*}
 \phi(|tp+(1-t)q|)
 &\leq\phi(t|p|+(1-t)|q|)\\
 &\leq t\phi(|p|)+(1-t)\phi(|q|)
\end{align*}
The triangle inequality for measures then gives
\begin{align*}
 J(tu+(1-t)v)&\leq tJ(u)+(1-t)J(v)\\
 F(tu+(1-t)v)
 &\leq tF(u)+(1-t)F(v)
       -\lambda t(1-t)\norm{K(u-v)}_2^2
\end{align*}
Hence $F$ is convex, and strictly convex if $K$ is injective.
In general, strict convexity is equivalent to strict convexity of $J$
on every nonconstant segment with $Ku=Kv$.

If $\phi$ is strictly convex, equality for $J$ forces
$\nabla u=\nabla v$ almost everywhere. Thus strict convexity holds
exactly when $Kw=0$ and $\nabla w=0$ almost everywhere force $w=0$
for $w\in X$. A nonzero such $w$ gives, for $s\geq0$,
\begin{align*}
 F(sw)&=|\Omega|\phi(0)+s\ell|D^sw|(\Omega)+\lambda\norm f_2^2
\end{align*}
On connected $\Omega$, this condition reduces to $K1\ne0$ on
$W^{1,1}(\Omega)\cap L^2(\Omega)$; on $\BV$, it must also exclude
nonzero functions with purely singular derivative.
\finish

\newpage
\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.2}
\begin{statement}
Assume that $\phi$ satisfies the conditions given in Exercise 3.1,
that it is differentiable, and that all quantities are well defined.
\par\smallskip
(a) Formally derive the following Euler-Lagrange equation associated
with the minimization of $F$ from (3.18):
\begin{align*}
 2\lambda K^*(Ku-f)
 &=\operatorname{div}\!\left(\frac{\phi'(|Du|)}{|Du|}Du\right)\\
 \frac{\phi'(|Du|)}{|Du|}Du\cdot\vec n
 &=0\big|_{\partial\Omega}
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(b) When $\phi$ is twice differentiable, show that the divergence
operator thus obtained can be expressed as
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{div}\!\left(\frac{\phi'(|Du|)}{|Du|}Du\right)
 &=\frac{\phi'(|Du|)}{|Du|}u_{\vec T\vec T}
   +\phi''(|Du|)u_{\vec N\vec N}
\end{align*}
at points $x\in\Omega$ with $|Du|(x)\ne0$, where
$\vec N=\frac{Du}{|Du|}$, $\vec T$ is orthogonal to $\vec N$ and
of unit length, and $u_{\vec T\vec T}=\vec T^{\,t}D^2u\vec T$,
$u_{\vec N\vec N}=\vec N^{\,t}D^2u\vec N$ are the second order
(directional) derivatives of $u$ in the $\vec T$ and $\vec N$
directions. Moreover, verify that
\begin{align*}
 u_{\vec T\vec T}+u_{\vec N\vec N}&=\Delta u
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(c) Give a geometric interpretation of the obtained diffusion
equation, function of the convexity of $\phi$.
\par\smallskip
(d) Under what conditions on $\phi$ does the quasilinear second order
operator
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{div}\!\left(\frac{\phi'(|Du|)}{|Du|}Du\right)
 &=\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}a_{i,j}(Du)u_{x_i,x_j}
\end{align*}
satisfy the degenerate ellipticity property
\begin{align*}
 \sum_{i,j=1}^{2}a_{i,j}(Du)\xi_i\xi_j&\ge0
\end{align*}
for all $\xi_1,\xi_2\in\mathbb R$?
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
Assume $u$ and $\partial\Omega$ are smooth. Set $r=|\nabla u|$.

\partlabel{a} For $A(p)=\phi'(|p|)p/|p|$ and a smooth variation $\eta$,
\begin{align*}
 \left.\frac{d}{dt}F(u+t\eta)\right|_{t=0}
 &=\int_\Omega A(\nabla u)\cdot\nabla\eta\dx
   +2\lambda\ip{Ku-f}{K\eta}\\
 &=\int_\Omega\bigl[-\diver A(\nabla u)
   +2\lambda K^*(Ku-f)\bigr]\eta\dx\\
 &\quad+\int_{\partial\Omega}A(\nabla u)\cdot n\,\eta\,dS
\end{align*}
Stationarity for arbitrary $\eta$ gives
\begin{align*}
 2\lambda K^*(Ku-f)&=\diver A(\nabla u)\\
 \left.A(\nabla u)\cdot n\right|_{\partial\Omega}&=0
\end{align*}
At zero, $p\mapsto\phi(|p|)$ is differentiable exactly when
$\phi'(0)=0$, with $A(0)=0$.

\partlabel{b} For $r>0$, set $N=\nabla u/r$, $H=D^2u$, and choose
unit $T\perp N$. Then
\begin{align*}
 u_{TT}+u_{NN}&=\operatorname{tr}H=\Delta u\\
 \nabla r&=H\nabla u/r\\
 \nabla r\cdot\nabla u&=r\,u_{NN}\\
 \diver A(\nabla u)
 &=\frac{\phi'(r)}r\Delta u
   +\left(\phi''(r)-\frac{\phi'(r)}r\right)u_{NN}\\
 &=\frac{\phi'(r)}r u_{TT}+\phi''(r)u_{NN}
\end{align*}

\partlabel{c} The gradient flow is
\begin{align*}
 u_t&=\frac{\phi'(r)}r u_{TT}+\phi''(r)u_{NN}
        -2\lambda K^*(Ku-f)
\end{align*}
The coefficients $\phi'(r)/r$ and $\phi''(r)$ govern diffusion
along and across level curves. Increasing convex $\phi$ makes both
nonnegative. Normal diffusion vanishes when $\phi''(r)=0$ and is
backward when $\phi''(r)<0$. For $\phi(r)=r$, only tangential
diffusion $u_{TT}/r=\diver N$ remains.

\partlabel{d} For $p\ne0$,
\begin{align*}
 a_{ij}(p)&=\frac{\phi'(|p|)}{|p|}\delta_{ij}
 +\left(\phi''(|p|)-\frac{\phi'(|p|)}{|p|}\right)
       \frac{p_ip_j}{|p|^2}\\
 \sum_{i,j=1}^2a_{ij}(p)\xi_i\xi_j
 &=\frac{\phi'(|p|)}{|p|}(\xi\cdot T)^2
   +\phi''(|p|)(\xi\cdot N)^2
\end{align*}
Here $N=p/|p|$ and $T\perp N$ is unit. Degenerate ellipticity
is equivalent to $\phi'(r)\geq0$ and $\phi''(r)\geq0$ for $r>0$.
If $\phi\in C^2([0,\infty))$ and $\phi'(0)=0$, then
$a(0)=\phi''(0)I\geq0$ is the continuous extension.
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.3}
\begin{statement}
Consider in two dimensions $f\in L^2(\Omega)$, $\lambda>0$, and
$u(\cdot,\lambda)\in BV(\Omega)$ the (unique) minimizer of the ROF
denoising functional
\begin{align*}
 F(u)&=\lambda\int_\Omega|Du|+\frac12\int_\Omega(f-u)^2\,dx
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(a) Show that the $L^2$-norm of $u(\cdot,\lambda)$ given by
$\sqrt{\int_\Omega u(x,\lambda)^2\,dx}$ is bounded from above by a
constant independent of $\lambda$.
\par\smallskip
(b) Show that
\begin{align*}
 \int_\Omega u(x,\lambda)\,dx&=\int_\Omega f(x)\,dx
\end{align*}
for any $\lambda>0$.
\par\smallskip
(c) Show that $u(\cdot,\lambda)$ converges in the $L^1(\Omega)$ norm
to the average of the data $f$, as $\lambda\to\infty$. In other
words, prove
\begin{align*}
 \lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\int_\Omega
 \left|u(x,\lambda)-\frac{\int_\Omega f(x)\,dx}{|\Omega|}\right|\,dx&=0
\end{align*}
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
Assume $\Omega$ is bounded, open, and connected for (c).
Set $u_\lambda=u(\cdot,\lambda)$ and
$\bar f=|\Omega|^{-1}\int_\Omega f\dx$.

\partlabel{a} Since $F(u_\lambda)\leq F(0)$,
\begin{align*}
 \frac12\norm{u_\lambda-f}_2^2&\leq\frac12\norm f_2^2\\
 \norm{u_\lambda}_2&\leq\norm{u_\lambda-f}_2+\norm f_2
 \leq2\norm f_2
\end{align*}

\partlabel{b} Since $\TV(u_\lambda+t)=\TV(u_\lambda)$,
\begin{align*}
 0&=\left.\frac{d}{dt}F(u_\lambda+t)\right|_{t=0}
   =\int_\Omega(u_\lambda-f)\dx
\end{align*}

\partlabel{c} Comparison with $\bar f$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \lambda\TV(u_\lambda)+\frac12\norm{u_\lambda-f}_2^2
 &\leq\frac12\norm{f-\bar f}_2^2\\
 \TV(u_\lambda)&\leq\frac{\norm{f-\bar f}_2^2}{2\lambda}
 \longrightarrow0
\end{align*}
Every sequence $\lambda_j\to\infty$ has a subsequence with
$u_{\lambda_j}\rightharpoonup w$ in $L^2$. For
$\psi\in C_c^1(\Omega;\R^2)$,
\begin{align*}
 \left|\int_\Omega u_{\lambda_j}\diver\psi\dx\right|
 &\leq\norm\psi_\infty\TV(u_{\lambda_j})\longrightarrow0
\end{align*}
Thus $Dw=0$; connectedness and (b) give $w=\bar f$.
The comparison inequality yields
\begin{align*}
 \frac12\norm{u_{\lambda_j}-\bar f}_2^2
 &\leq\ip{f-\bar f}{u_{\lambda_j}-\bar f}\longrightarrow0
\end{align*}
The subsequence criterion gives $u_\lambda\to\bar f$ in $L^2$.
Hence
\begin{align*}
 \norm{u_\lambda-\bar f}_1
 &\leq|\Omega|^{1/2}\norm{u_\lambda-\bar f}_2\longrightarrow0
\end{align*}
\finish


\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.4}
\begin{statement}
Consider in two dimensions $f\in L^2(\R^2)$, $\lambda>0$, and the ROF
denoising functional
\begin{align*}
 F(u)=\lambda\int |Du|+\frac12\int(f-u)^2\dx
\end{align*}
Show the following invariance properties:
\par\smallskip
(a) For any constant $c$, if $u$ is a minimizer of $F$ with data $f$,
then $u+c$ is a minimizer of $F$ with data $f+c$ (gray-level invariance).
\par\smallskip
(b) For any translation $\tau_h$ defined by
$(\tau_h)(f)(x)=f(x+h)$, $h\in\R^2$, if $u$ is a minimizer of $F$
with data $f$, then $\tau_h(u)$ is a minimizer of $F$ with data
$\tau_h(f)$ (translation invariance).
\par\smallskip
(c) Let $R$ be an isometry of $\R^2$ and define $(Rf)(x)=f(Rx)$.
If $u$ is a minimizer of $F$ with data $f$, then $Ru$ is a minimizer
with data $Rf$ (isometry invariance).
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
Set $\mathcal A=\BV(\R^2)\cap L^2(\R^2)$ and
$E_f(v)=\lambda|Dv|(\R^2)+\frac12\norm{f-v}_2^2$.

\partlabel{a}
For $w=v+c\in c+\mathcal A$,
\begin{align*}
 D(v+c)&=Dv\\
 E_{f+c}(u+c)&=E_f(u)\le E_f(v)=E_{f+c}(w)
\end{align*}
For $c\ne0$, $f+c\notin L^2(\R^2)$, so the shifted problem
uses the affine class $c+\mathcal A$.

\partlabel{b}
Changing variables in the fidelity integral and the dual definition
of total variation gives
\begin{align*}
 \norm{\tau_h f-\tau_h v}_2&=\norm{f-v}_2\\
 |D(\tau_h v)|(\R^2)&=|Dv|(\R^2)\\
 E_{\tau_h f}(\tau_h u)&=E_f(u)\le E_f(v)=E_{\tau_h f}(\tau_h v)
\end{align*}
Here $\tau_h$ maps $\mathcal A$ bijectively onto itself.

\partlabel{c}
Write $S(x)=Qx+b$ with $Q^tQ=I$.
The map $\phi\mapsto\widetilde\phi=Q\phi\circ S^{-1}$ is a
bijection of the admissible test fields, and
\begin{align*}
 \diver\widetilde\phi&=(\diver\phi)\circ S^{-1}\\
 \int_{\R^2}v(Sx)\diver\phi(x)\dx
 &=\int_{\R^2}v(y)\diver\widetilde\phi(y)\,dy
\end{align*}
The map $v\mapsto v\circ S$ is a bijection of $\mathcal A$.
Taking suprema and changing variables gives
\begin{align*}
 |D(v\circ S)|(\R^2)&=|Dv|(\R^2)\\
 \norm{f\circ S-v\circ S}_2&=\norm{f-v}_2\\
 E_{f\circ S}(u\circ S)&=E_f(u)\le E_f(v)=E_{f\circ S}(v\circ S)
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.5}
\begin{statement}
Let $\alpha>0$, $\lambda>0$ and $f:\R^2\mapsto\R$ be defined by
$f(x)=\alpha\chi_D$, where $D=B(0,R)$ is the open disk centered at
the origin of radius $R>0$. Consider
\begin{align*}
 F(u)=\lambda\int_{\R^2}(f-u)^2\dx+\int_{\R^2}|Du|
\end{align*}
for all $u\in\BV(\R^2)$.

Show in an elementary way that $f$ cannot be a minimizer of $F$
over $\BV(\R^2)$.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
For $0<t<\min\{\alpha,2/(\lambda R)\}$, set $v_t=(\alpha-t)\chi_D$.
Then
\begin{align*}
 F(v_t)-F(f)&=\lambda\pi R^2t^2-2\pi Rt\\
 &=\pi Rt(\lambda Rt-2)<0
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.6}
\begin{statement}
Let $\Omega$ be an open, bounded and connected subset of $\R^2$,
$\lambda>0$, $f\in L^2(\Omega)$, and $u_\lambda$ be a solution of
\begin{align*}
 \inf_{v\in\BV(\Omega)}\int_\Omega|Dv|
       +\lambda\int_\Omega(Kv-f)^2\dx
\end{align*}
where $K:L^2(\Omega)\to L^2(\Omega)$ is linear and continuous.
Show that the function $\sigma(\lambda)=\|Ku_\lambda-f\|_{L^2(\Omega)}$
is well defined and decreasing.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
Work on $\BV(\Omega)\cap L^2(\Omega)$ and denote the objective by
$F_\lambda$. For minimizers $u,v$ with value $m$, convexity gives
\begin{align*}
 m\le F_\lambda\!\left(\frac{u+v}{2}\right)
 &\le m-\frac\lambda4\norm{Ku-Kv}_2^2
\end{align*}
Thus $Ku=Kv$, so $\sigma(\lambda)$ is well defined.

For $0<\lambda_1<\lambda_2$, set $u_i=u_{\lambda_i}$,
$a_i=\TV(u_i)$, and $s_i=\norm{Ku_i-f}_2$. Minimality gives
\begin{align*}
 a_1+\lambda_1s_1^2&\le a_2+\lambda_1s_2^2\\
 a_2+\lambda_2s_2^2&\le a_1+\lambda_2s_1^2\\
 (\lambda_2-\lambda_1)(s_2^2-s_1^2)&\le0\\
 \sigma(\lambda_2)&\le\sigma(\lambda_1)
\end{align*}
\finish

\newpage
\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.7}
\begin{statement}
Let $\epsilon>0$ and $\Omega\subset\mathbb R^N$ be an open and bounded subset of $\mathbb R^N$.
\par\smallskip
(a) Show that
\begin{align*}
\sqrt{\epsilon^2+|x|^2}
&=\sup\left\{x\cdot y+\sqrt{\epsilon^2(1-|y|^2)}:
        y\in\mathbb R^N,\ |y|\le1\right\}
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(b) For $u\in L^1(\Omega)$, define
\begin{align*}
J_0(u)
&=\sup\left\{\int_\Omega u\operatorname{div}\phi\,dx
       \;\middle|\;\phi\in C_c^1(\Omega;\mathbb R^N),\ |\phi|\le1\right\}
\end{align*}
and
\begin{align*}
J_\epsilon(u)
&=\sup\left\{\int_\Omega
 \left(u\operatorname{div}\phi+\sqrt{\epsilon^2(1-|\phi(x)|^2)}\right)dx
 \;\middle|\;\phi\in C_c^1(\Omega;\mathbb R^N),\ |\phi|\le1\right\}
\end{align*}
($J_\epsilon(u)=\int_\Omega\sqrt{\epsilon^2+|Du|^2}$).
For any $\epsilon>0$ and $u\in L^1(\Omega)$, show that $J_0(u)<\infty$
if and only if $J_\epsilon(u)<\infty$. Moreover, for any $u\in BV(\Omega)$,
\begin{align*}
\lim_{\epsilon\to0}J_\epsilon(u)&=J_0(u)
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(c) For any $\epsilon\ge0$, $J_\epsilon$ is (weakly) lower semicontinuous with respect to the weak $L^p$ topology for $1\le p<\infty$.
\par\smallskip
(d) For any $\epsilon\ge0$, $J_\epsilon$ is convex.
\par\smallskip
(e) For any $\epsilon\ge0$, $J_\epsilon$ is not a strictly convex function.
\par\smallskip
Note that, if $u\in BV(\Omega)$, then $J_\epsilon(u)$ coincides with
$\int_\Omega\sqrt{\epsilon^2+|Du|^2}$ which is well defined also based on the notion of convex functions of measure [126].
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
\partlabel{a}
For $|y|\le1$, Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\begin{align*}
 x\cdot y+\eps\sqrt{1-|y|^2}
 &=(x,\eps)\cdot\bigl(y,\sqrt{1-|y|^2}\bigr)\\
 &\le\sqrt{|x|^2+\eps^2}
\end{align*}
Equality holds at $y=x/\sqrt{|x|^2+\eps^2}$.

\partlabel{b}
For admissible $\phi$,
\begin{align*}
 0\le\eps\int_\Omega\sqrt{1-|\phi|^2}\dx&\le\eps|\Omega|\\
 J_0(u)&\le J_\eps(u)\le J_0(u)+\eps|\Omega|
\end{align*}
Both are finite exactly when $u\in\BV(\Omega)$, in which case
\begin{align*}
 0\le J_\eps(u)-J_0(u)&\le\eps|\Omega|\longrightarrow0
\end{align*}

\partlabel{c}
Set
\begin{align*}
 A_{\eps,\phi}(u)&=\int_\Omega u\diver\phi\dx
           +\eps\int_\Omega\sqrt{1-|\phi|^2}\dx
\end{align*}
Since $\diver\phi\in L^{p'}(\Omega)$, $A_{\eps,\phi}$ is weakly
continuous. Hence each sublevel set
\begin{align*}
 \{u:J_\eps(u)\le a\}
 &=\bigcap_{\phi}\{u:A_{\eps,\phi}(u)\le a\}
\end{align*}
is weakly closed.

\partlabel{d}
For $0<t<1$,
\begin{align*}
 J_\eps(tu+(1-t)v)
 &=\sup_\phi\bigl[tA_{\eps,\phi}(u)+(1-t)A_{\eps,\phi}(v)\bigr]\\
 &\le tJ_\eps(u)+(1-t)J_\eps(v)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{e}
For constants $c$, $\int_\Omega c\diver\phi\dx=0$.
The bound $\sqrt{1-|\phi|^2}\le1$ and the choice $\phi=0$ give
$J_\eps(c)=\eps|\Omega|$. For distinct constants $c_1,c_2$ on
nonempty $\Omega$ and $0<t<1$,
\begin{align*}
 J_\eps(tc_1+(1-t)c_2)
 &=\eps|\Omega|=tJ_\eps(c_1)+(1-t)J_\eps(c_2)
\end{align*}
\finish


\newpage
\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.8}
\begin{statement}
In the purely denoising case ($K=I$), show that the stationary semi-implicit scheme presented in Section 3.1.6 is unconditionally stable in the $L^\infty$-norm.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
For an interior grid point $p$, let $q_1,\ldots,q_4$ be its
neighbors in the order of Section 3.1.6. Set
\begin{align*}
 a^n_{p,k}&=\frac{c_k(u^n)}{2\lambda h^2}>0\\
 u^{n+1}_p
 &=\frac{f_p+\sum_{k=1}^4a^n_{p,k}u^n_{q_k}}
          {1+\sum_{k=1}^4a^n_{p,k}}
\end{align*}
This is a convex combination. The boundary rule copies interior
values, including diagonal neighbors at corners. With
$m_f=\min_p f_p$, $M_f=\max_p f_p$, and $u^0=f$, induction gives,
for every $h,\lambda,\eps>0$ and $n\ge0$,
\begin{align*}
 m_f&\le u^n_p\le M_f\\
 \sup_{n\ge0}\norm{u^n}_{\ell^\infty}
 &\le\norm{f}_{\ell^\infty}
\end{align*}
\finish

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{3.9}
\begin{statement}
In the purely denoising case ($K=I$), show that the evolutionary semi-implicit scheme presented in Section 3.1.6 is unconditionally stable in the $L^\infty$-norm.
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}

\solution
With the notation of Exercise 3.8 and $\tau=\Delta t>0$,
\begin{align*}
 u^{n+1}_p
 &=\frac{u^n_p+\tau f_p+\tau\sum_{k=1}^4a^n_{p,k}u^n_{q_k}}
          {1+\tau+\tau\sum_{k=1}^4a^n_{p,k}}
\end{align*}
This is a convex combination for every $\tau>0$. The same induction
gives, for every $h,\lambda,\eps,\tau>0$ and $n\ge0$,
\begin{align*}
 m_f&\le u^n_p\le M_f\\
 \sup_{n\ge0}\norm{u^n}_{\ell^\infty}
 &\le\norm{f}_{\ell^\infty}
\end{align*}
\finish

\clearpage
\pdfbookmark[0]{Chapter 4}{chapter4}
\begin{center}
\textbf{Chapter 4}
\end{center}

\exercise{4.1}
\begin{statement}
Prove Proposition 3.
\end{statement}
\solution
Set $J(v)=|v|_{NL/TV}$ and $F(v)=\lambda\norm{f-Kv}_2^2+J(v)$.

\partlabel{i}
If $\norm{K^*f}_*\leq(2\lambda)^{-1}$, then
\begin{align*}
 F(v)-F(0)
 &=\lambda\norm{Kv}_2^2-2\lambda\ip{K^*f}{v}+J(v)\\
 &\geq\lambda\norm{Kv}_2^2\geq0
\end{align*}
If $0$ minimizes $F$, then for $t>0$,
\begin{align*}
 0&\leq F(tv)-F(0)\\
 &=\lambda t^2\norm{Kv}_2^2-2\lambda t\ip{K^*f}{v}+tJ(v)
\end{align*}
Dividing by $t$ and taking $t\downarrow0$ gives
\begin{align*}
 2\lambda\ip{K^*f}{v}&\leq J(v)\\
 \norm{K^*f}_*&\leq\frac1{2\lambda}
\end{align*}

\partlabel{ii}
Assume $(2\lambda)^{-1}<\norm{K^*f}_*<\infty$.
For a minimizer $u$, set $q=K^*(f-Ku)$. For $t>0$,
\begin{align*}
 0&\leq F(u+th)-F(u)\\
 &\leq-2\lambda t\ip{q}{h}+\lambda t^2\norm{Kh}_2^2+tJ(h)
\end{align*}
Dividing by $t$ and taking $t\downarrow0$ gives
\begin{align*}
 2\lambda\ip{q}{h}&\leq J(h)\\
 \norm{q}_*&\leq\frac1{2\lambda}
\end{align*}
Since $J((1+t)u)=(1+t)J(u)$ for $|t|<1$,
\begin{align*}
 0&=\left.\frac{d}{dt}F((1+t)u)\right|_{t=0}\\
 &=-2\lambda\ip{q}{u}+J(u)
\end{align*}
If $J(u)=0$, dual boundedness gives $\ip{K^*f}{u}=0$, hence
\begin{align*}
 \norm{Ku}_2^2&=\ip{K^*f}{u}-\ip{q}{u}=0\\
 q&=K^*f
\end{align*}
This contradicts $\norm{q}_*\leq(2\lambda)^{-1}$. Hence
\begin{align*}
 \norm{q}_*&\geq\frac{\ip{q}{u}}{J(u)}=\frac1{2\lambda}
\end{align*}
Conversely, suppose
\begin{align*}
 \norm{q}_*&=\frac1{2\lambda}\\
 \ip{q}{u}&=\frac{J(u)}{2\lambda}
\end{align*}
For every $v$,
\begin{align*}
 F(v)-F(u)
 &=\lambda\norm{K(v-u)}_2^2-2\lambda\ip{q}{v-u}+J(v)-J(u)\\
 &=\lambda\norm{K(v-u)}_2^2+J(v)-2\lambda\ip{q}{v}\\
 &\geq\lambda\norm{K(v-u)}_2^2\geq0
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{4.2}
\begin{statement}
Show the adjoint relation
\begin{align*}
 \ip{\nabla_wu}{\vec v}&=\ip{u}{-\diver_w\vec v}
\end{align*}
\end{statement}
\solution
By Fubini and $w(x,y)=w(y,x)$,
\begin{align*}
 \ip{\nabla_wu}{\vec v}
 &=\int_\Omega\int_\Omega
   (u(y)-u(x))v(x,y)\sqrt{w(x,y)}\,dy\,dx\\
 &=\int_\Omega\int_\Omega
   u(x)(v(y,x)-v(x,y))\sqrt{w(x,y)}\,dy\,dx\\
 &=\ip{u}{-\diver_w\vec v}
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}
\end{samepage}

\clearpage
\begin{samepage}
\exercise{4.3}
\begin{statement}
Show (as a version of the classical divergence theorem):
\begin{align*}
 \int_\Omega\diver_w\vec v\,dx&=0
\end{align*}
\end{statement}
\solution
By Exercise 4.2,
\begin{align*}
 \int_\Omega\diver_w\vec v\,dx
 &=-\ip{\nabla_w1}{\vec v}\\
 &=0
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}
\end{samepage}

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{4.4}
\begin{statement}
Show that the nonlocal Laplacian $\Delta_w$ is self adjoint:
\begin{align*}
 \ip{\Delta_wu}{v}&=\ip{u}{\Delta_wv}
\end{align*}
\end{statement}
\solution
Using $\Delta_w=\tfrac12\diver_w\nabla_w$ and Exercise 4.2,
\begin{align*}
 \ip{\Delta_wu}{v}
 &=-\frac12\ip{\nabla_wu}{\nabla_wv}\\
 &=\frac12\ip{u}{\diver_w\nabla_wv}\\
 &=\ip{u}{\Delta_wv}
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}
\end{samepage}

\begin{samepage}
\exercise{4.5}
\begin{statement}
Show that the nonlocal Laplacian $\Delta_w$ is negative semidefinite:
\begin{align*}
 \ip{\Delta_wu}{u}&=-\ip{\nabla_wu}{\nabla_wu}
\end{align*}
\end{statement}
\solution
The definition $\Delta_w=\tfrac12\diver_w\nabla_w$ requires the factor $1/2$:
\begin{align*}
 \ip{\Delta_wu}{u}
 &=-\frac12\ip{\nabla_wu}{\nabla_wu}\\
 &=-\frac12\int_\Omega\int_\Omega
   (u(y)-u(x))^2w(x,y)\,dy\,dx\\
 &\leq0
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}
\end{samepage}

\clearpage
\begin{samepage}
\exercise{4.6}
\begin{statement}
Using the notations from this chapter, consider the difference-based
regularizing functional
\begin{align*}
 \Phi(u)&=\int_{\Omega\times\Omega}
 \phi\bigl((u(y)-u(x))^2w(x,y)\bigr)\,dy\,dx
\end{align*}
Assuming that $\phi$ is differentiable, compute the Euler--Lagrange
equation associated with the minimization with respect to $u$ of
\begin{align*}
 \Phi(u)+\lambda\norm{f-u}_{L^2(\Omega)}^2
\end{align*}
\end{statement}
\end{samepage}
\solution
Set $E(u)=\Phi(u)+\lambda\norm{f-u}_2^2$ and
\begin{align*}
 a_u(x,y)&=w(x,y)\phi'\bigl((u(y)-u(x))^2w(x,y)\bigr)
\end{align*}
By $a_u(x,y)=a_u(y,x)$,
\begin{align*}
 D\Phi(u)[h]
 &=2\int_\Omega\int_\Omega
   a_u(x,y)(u(y)-u(x))(h(y)-h(x))\,dy\,dx\\
 &=4\int_\Omega h(x)\int_\Omega
   a_u(x,y)(u(x)-u(y))\,dy\,dx
\end{align*}
The equation $DE(u)=0$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \lambda(u(x)-f(x))
 +2\int_\Omega a_u(x,y)(u(x)-u(y))\,dy&=0
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}

\clearpage
\exercise{4.7}
\begin{statement}
In two dimensions, let $f$ be a given noisy image degraded by additive
Gaussian noise of zero mean. Apply the following minimization in $u$
with $NL/H^1$ regularization for denoising the image $f$:
\begin{align*}
 \inf_u\left\{\lambda\norm{f-u}_{L^2(\Omega)}^2
       +\int_\Omega|\nabla_wu|^2\,dx\right\}
\end{align*}
Compute the associated Euler--Lagrange equation and give its discretization
and numerical algorithm using gradient descent. Repeat the same exercise
using $NL/TV$ regularization instead and compare the results.
\end{statement}
\solution
Keep $w$ fixed and symmetric.

\partlabel{$NL/H^1$}
By Exercise 4.2,
\begin{align*}
 DE(u)[h]
 &=2\lambda\ip{u-f}{h}+2\ip{\nabla_wu}{\nabla_wh}\\
 &=\ip{2\lambda(u-f)-4\Delta_wu}{h}
\\[3pt]
 \lambda(u-f)-2\Delta_wu&=0\\
 u_t&=4\Delta_wu-2\lambda(u-f)
\end{align*}
For pixel area $a$, set $w_{ij}=a\,w(x_i,x_j)$ and $w_{ii}=0$.
Dividing the discrete energy by $a$ gives
\begin{align*}
 E_H(u)&=\lambda\sum_i(u_i-f_i)^2
          +\sum_{i,j}w_{ij}(u_j-u_i)^2
\\[3pt]
 (g_H(u))_i&=2\lambda(u_i-f_i)+4\sum_jw_{ij}(u_i-u_j)\\
 (\lambda+2d_i)u_i-2\sum_jw_{ij}u_j&=\lambda f_i\\
 u_i^{k+1}&=u_i^k-\tau_k(g_H(u^k))_i
\end{align*}
Here $d_i=\sum_jw_{ij}$ and $u^0=f$.
Since $\norm{D^2E_H}\leq2\lambda+8\max_i d_i$, a constant step
\begin{align*}
 0<\tau&<\frac1{\lambda+4\max_i d_i}
\end{align*}
gives convergence to the unique minimizer.

\clearpage
\partlabel{$NL/TV$}
For $\eps>0$, put
\begin{align*}
 r_\eps(x)&=\sqrt{\eps^2+\int_\Omega(u(y)-u(x))^2w(x,y)\,dy}\\
 E_\eps(u)&=\lambda\norm{u-f}_2^2+\int_\Omega r_\eps(x)\,dx
\\[3pt]
 DE_\eps(u)[h]
 &=2\lambda\ip{u-f}{h}
   +\ip{\nabla_wu/r_\eps}{\nabla_wh}\\
 &=\ip{2\lambda(u-f)-\diver_w(\nabla_wu/r_\eps)}{h}
\\[3pt]
 2\lambda(u(x)-f(x))
 &=\int_\Omega w(x,y)(u(y)-u(x))
       \left(\frac1{r_\eps(x)}+\frac1{r_\eps(y)}\right)dy\\
 u_t&=\diver_w(\nabla_wu/r_\eps)-2\lambda(u-f)
\end{align*}
For the unsmoothed energy, use $\eps=0$ where $r_0>0$.
At $r_0(x)=0$, replace $\nabla_wu/r_0$ by a field $p$ satisfying
$\norm{p(x,\cdot)}_2\leq1$. The optimality equation is
$2\lambda(u-f)-\diver_wp=0$.

Discretization gives
\begin{align*}
 r_i(u)&=\sqrt{\eps^2+\sum_jw_{ij}(u_j-u_i)^2}\\
 E_T(u)&=\lambda\sum_i(u_i-f_i)^2+\sum_i r_i(u)\\
 (g_T(u))_i
 &=2\lambda(u_i-f_i)
   +\sum_jw_{ij}(u_i-u_j)\left(\frac1{r_i(u)}+\frac1{r_j(u)}\right)\\
 u_i^{k+1}&=u_i^k-\tau_k(g_T(u^k))_i
\end{align*}
For $u^0=f$, a constant step
\begin{align*}
 0<\tau&<\frac1{\lambda+2\max_i d_i/\eps}
\end{align*}
gives convergence to the unique smoothed minimizer.
\begin{align*}
 0\leq\int_\Omega r_\eps\,dx-\int_\Omega|\nabla_wu|\,dx
 &\leq\eps|\Omega|
\end{align*}

\clearpage
\partlabel{Numerical comparison}
Use the $48\times48$ image below, unit pixel area, and independent
Gaussian noise with standard deviation $0.1$ (NumPy seed $197$).
Fix weights from $3\times3$ patches of $f$ in an $11\times11$ window:
\begin{align*}
 d_{ij}&=\frac19\sum_{s\in\{-1,0,1\}^2}(f_{i+s}-f_{j+s})^2\\
 w_{ij}&=\exp(-d_{ij}/0.2^2)
\end{align*}
Set diagonal and outside-window weights to zero. Reflect boundary samples.
Take $\lambda_H=5$, $\lambda_T=15$, and $\eps=10^{-3}$.

Start at $(2\lambda)^{-1}$, then at
$\min\{1.5\tau_{k-1},(2\lambda)^{-1}\}$. With $g^k=\nabla E(u^k)$, halve the step until
\begin{align*}
 E(u^k-\tau_k g^k)&\leq E(u^k)-10^{-4}\tau_k\norm{g^k}_2^2
\end{align*}
Stop when $\norm{g^k}_2\leq10^{-6}\norm{g^0}_2$.
For the clean image $u^*$,
\begin{align*}
 \mathrm{RMSE}&=\sqrt{\frac1{48^2}\sum_i(u_i-u_i^*)^2}\\
 \mathrm{PSNR}&=20\log_{10}(1/\mathrm{RMSE})
\end{align*}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
Method & Iterations & RMSE & PSNR (dB)\\
\hline
Noisy image & -- & 0.10048 & 19.96\\
$NL/H^1$ & 148 & 0.09639 & 20.32\\
$NL/TV_\eps$ & 2506 & 0.07853 & 22.10\\
\end{tabular}

\pdfbookmark[0]{Chapter 5}{chapter5}
\begin{center}
\textbf{Chapter 5}
\end{center}

\exercise{5.1}
\begin{statement}
Recall in two dimensions that $G$ denotes the Banach space consisting of
all generalized functions $v$ that can be written as:
\begin{align*}
 v(x_1,x_2)&=\partial_{x_1}g_1(x_1,x_2)+\partial_{x_2}g_2(x_1,x_2)\\
 g_1,g_2&\in L^\infty(\R^2)
\end{align*}
induced by the norm $\norm{\cdot}_G$ defined as the lower bound of all
$L^\infty$ norms of the functions $|\vec g|$ where $\vec g=(g_1,g_2)$,
$|\vec g(x_1,x_2)|=\sqrt{g_1(x_1,x_2)^2+g_2(x_1,x_2)^2}$ and where
the infimum is computed over all decompositions of $v$ into
$v=\diver\vec g$.

Recall in two dimensions the cartoon+texture model from (5.3) as an
approximation of Meyer's $(BV,G)$ model:
\begin{align*}
 \inf_{u,g_1,g_2}\biggl\{G_p(u,g_1,g_2)
 &=\int|Du|+\mu\int|f-u-\diver\vec g|^2\,dx\\
 &\quad+\lambda\left[\int\left(\sqrt{g_1^2+g_2^2}\right)^pdx\right]^{1/p}
 \biggr\}
 \tag{5.12}
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(a) Compute the Euler--Lagrange equations related to (5.12).
\par\smallskip
(b) In the standard Rudin--Osher--Fatemi model (5.1), when the minimizer
is assumed to be in $W^{1,1}(\R^2)$, the residual is given by
$f-u=-\frac1{2\lambda}K(u)$, with $K(u)$ the curvature operator
defined by $K(u)=\diver\left(\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\right)$.
Give the residual $f-(u+v)=f-(u+\diver\vec g)$ of model (5.12).
\par\smallskip
(c) Prove that for $p=1$, $|\nabla K(u)|=\lambda$.
\par\smallskip
(d) Prove, for $p>1$, that:
\begin{align*}
 \lambda\left(
 \frac{\sqrt{g_1^2+g_2^2}}{\norm{\sqrt{g_1^2+g_2^2}}_{L^p}}
 \right)^{p-1}&=|\nabla K(u)|\\
 \lambda\norm{\sqrt{g_1^2+g_2^2}}_{L^p}
 &=\int\sqrt{g_1^2+g_2^2}\,|\nabla K(u)|
\end{align*}
One can check that if $\vec g$ is not of constant magnitude, then
$\int\sqrt{g_1^2+g_2^2}\,|\nabla K(u)|$ can be viewed as a weighted
total variation of the curvature $K(u)$.
\par\smallskip
(e) Prove that if $(\hat u=0,\hat{\vec g}\in(L^p)^2)$ is a minimizer
of (5.12), then
\begin{align*}
 \norm{f-\diver\hat{\vec g}}_G&\leq\frac1{2\mu}
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(f) Prove that if $(\hat u\in BV,\hat{\vec g}=(0,0))$ is a minimizer
of (5.12), then
\begin{align*}
 \norm{\nabla(\hat u-f)}_{L^q}&\leq\frac\lambda{2\mu}
\end{align*}
with $\frac1p+\frac1q=1$.
\par\smallskip
(g) Prove that $(\hat u=0,\hat{\vec g}=(0,0))$ is a minimizer of
(5.12) if and only if
\begin{align*}
 \norm f_G&\leq\frac1{2\mu}\\
 \norm{\nabla f}_{L^q}&\leq\frac\lambda{2\mu}
\end{align*}
\end{statement}

\solution
Take $1\leq p<\infty$, $f\in L^2(\R^2)$, $u\in BV\cap L^2$,
and $\vec g\in L^p$ with $\diver\vec g\in L^2$. Set
\begin{align*}
 r&=f-u-\diver\vec g\\
 A&=\norm{|\vec g|}_{L^p}\\
 G_p(u,\vec g)&=\TV(u)+\mu\norm r_2^2+\lambda A
\end{align*}

\partlabel{a}
Assume smoothness and $|\nabla u|>0$. For $p>1$, $A>0$, and
compactly supported variations,
\begin{align*}
 D_uG_p[h]
 &=\int\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\cdot\nabla h-2\mu\int rh\\
 &=\int(-K(u)-2\mu r)h\\
 D_{\vec g}G_p[\vec k]
 &=-2\mu\int r\diver\vec k
   +\lambda A^{1-p}\int|\vec g|^{p-2}\vec g\cdot\vec k\\
 &=\int\left(2\mu\nabla r
       +\lambda A^{1-p}|\vec g|^{p-2}\vec g\right)\cdot\vec k
\end{align*}
Thus, for $i=1,2$,
\begin{align*}
 K(u)+2\mu r&=0\\
 2\mu\partial_{x_i}r
   +\lambda A^{1-p}|\vec g|^{p-2}g_i&=0
\end{align*}
Set $|\vec g|^{p-2}\vec g=0$ at $\vec g=0$. For $p=1$, replace
$A^{1-p}|\vec g|^{p-2}\vec g$ by $\vec\eta=\vec g/|\vec g|$
on $\{\vec g\ne0\}$ and $|\vec\eta|\leq1$ elsewhere.
For $p>1$ and $A=0$, use $\norm{\vec\eta}_{L^q}\leq1$.
For nonsmooth $u$, use $2\mu r\in\partial\TV(u)$.

\partlabel{b}
\begin{align*}
 f-(u+\diver\vec g)&=-\frac1{2\mu}K(u)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{c}
By (a) and (b),
\begin{align*}
 \nabla K(u)&=\lambda\vec\eta
\end{align*}
Hence $|\nabla K(u)|=\lambda$ on $\{\vec g\ne0\}$ and
$|\nabla K(u)|\leq\lambda$ elsewhere. The printed equality needs
this restriction.

\partlabel{d}
For $p>1$ and $A>0$,
\begin{align*}
 \nabla K(u)&=\lambda A^{1-p}|\vec g|^{p-2}\vec g\\
 |\nabla K(u)|&=\lambda\left(\frac{|\vec g|}{A}\right)^{p-1}\\
 \int|\vec g|\,|\nabla K(u)|
 &=\lambda A^{1-p}\int|\vec g|^p\\
 &=\lambda A
\end{align*}
For $A=0$, use $\norm{\nabla K(u)}_{L^q}\leq\lambda$.

\partlabel{e}
Set $r=f-\diver\hat{\vec g}$.
For $h\in C_c^\infty$ and $t>0$, minimality gives
\begin{align*}
 0&\leq G_p(th,\hat{\vec g})-G_p(0,\hat{\vec g})\\
 &=t\TV(h)-2\mu t\ip rh+\mu t^2\norm h_2^2
\end{align*}
Letting $t\downarrow0$ and using $\pm h$,
\begin{align*}
 |\ip rh|&\leq\frac1{2\mu}\norm{\nabla h}_1
\end{align*}
Hahn--Banach extends $T(\nabla h)=\ip rh$ to $L^1$.
By $(L^1)^*=L^\infty$, there is $b$ with
\begin{align*}
 \norm b_\infty&\leq\frac1{2\mu}\\
 \ip rh&=\int b\cdot\nabla h\\
 r&=\diver(-b)\\
 \norm r_G&\leq\frac1{2\mu}
\end{align*}

\clearpage
\partlabel{f}
Set $r=f-\hat u$. For $\vec k\in C_c^\infty(\R^2;\R^2)$ and $t>0$,
\begin{align*}
 0&\leq G_p(\hat u,t\vec k)-G_p(\hat u,0)\\
 &=\lambda t\norm{\vec k}_p
   -2\mu t\ip r{\diver\vec k}
   +\mu t^2\norm{\diver\vec k}_2^2
\end{align*}
Letting $t\downarrow0$ and using $\pm\vec k$,
\begin{align*}
 |\ip{\nabla r}{\vec k}|
 &=|\ip r{\diver\vec k}|\\
 &\leq\frac\lambda{2\mu}\norm{\vec k}_p
\end{align*}
By $L^p$ duality,
\begin{align*}
 \norm{\nabla(\hat u-f)}_{L^q}
 &=\norm{\nabla r}_{L^q}\leq\frac\lambda{2\mu}
\end{align*}

\partlabel{g}
Necessity follows from (e)--(f). Conversely, integration by parts,
the definition of $\norm f_G$, and smooth approximation give
\begin{align*}
 2\mu\ip fu&\leq\TV(u)\\
 2\mu\ip f{\diver\vec g}
 &=-2\mu\int\nabla f\cdot\vec g\\
 &\leq2\mu\norm{\nabla f}_q\norm{\vec g}_p\\
 &\leq\lambda\norm{\vec g}_p
\end{align*}
Therefore
\begin{align*}
 G_p(u,\vec g)-G_p(0,0)
 &=\TV(u)+\lambda\norm{\vec g}_p
      +\mu\norm{u+\diver\vec g}_2^2\\
 &\quad-2\mu\ip f{u+\diver\vec g}\\
 &\geq\mu\norm{u+\diver\vec g}_2^2\geq0
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}


\clearpage
\exercise{5.2}
\begin{statement}
For $f\in L^2(\Omega)$ with $\Omega\subset\R^2$ open, bounded,
connected and with Lipschitz boundary $\partial\Omega$, recall the
decomposition model (5.4),
\begin{align*}
 \inf_u E(u)
 &=\int_\Omega|Du|
   +\lambda\int_\Omega|\nabla(\Delta^{-1})(f-u)|^2\,dx
 \tag{5.13}
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(a) In this question, we check that the last component of (5.13)
makes sense. Let
\begin{align*}
 V_0&=\left\{P\in H^1(\Omega):\int_\Omega P(x)\,dx=0\right\}
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(i) Prove that $V_0$, as a closed vector subspace of $H^1(\Omega)$,
is a Hilbert space for the reduced norm
$|P|_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}:=\norm{\nabla P}_{L^2(\Omega)}$.
\par\smallskip
(ii) Using Lax--Milgram theorem, deduce that if $v\in L^2(\Omega)$
with $\int_\Omega v(x)\,dx=0$, then the problem
\begin{align*}
 -\Delta P&=v\\
 \left.\frac{\partial P}{\partial n}\right|_{\partial\Omega}&=0
\end{align*}
with $n$ the outward unit vector normal to $\Omega$, admits a unique
solution $P$ in $V_0$.
\par\smallskip
(b) Assuming that $u\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)$, derive the Euler--Lagrange
equation $E_u=0$ associated with (5.13).
\par\smallskip
(c) Instead of directly solving the obtained Euler--Lagrange equation
with gradient descent (that is, $u_t=-E_u$), apply the Laplacian
operator to the Euler--Lagrange equation, to solve $u_t=\Delta E_u$.
Derive this evolution problem obtained after applying a gradient
descent method. Show in particular that the assumption
$\int_\Omega v\,dx=\int_\Omega(f-u)\,dx=0$ is fulfilled throughout
the descent process.
\par\smallskip
(d) Prove that the new flow $u_t=\Delta E_u$ decreases the energy.
\par\smallskip
(e) Let us consider the problem:
\begin{align*}
 \inf_{u\in BV(\Omega)}\biggl\{E(u)
 &=\int_\Omega|Du|
   +\lambda\int_\Omega|\nabla(\Delta^{-1})(f-u)|^2\,dx\\
 &\qquad\int_\Omega(f-u)\,dx=0\biggr\}
 \tag{5.14}
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(i) Justify that the infimum is finite.
\par\smallskip
(ii) Let $(u_n)_n$ be a minimizing sequence of (5.14). Using
Poincar\'e--Wirtinger's inequality and the embedding
$L^2(\Omega)\subset L^1(\Omega)$, prove that $(u_n)_n$ is uniformly
bounded in $BV(\Omega)$. Therefore, there is a subsequence of
$(u_n)_n$, still denoted $(u_n)_n$, and an element $u\in BV(\Omega)$
such that $u_n\to u$ strongly in $L^1(\Omega)$ and
\begin{align*}
 \int_\Omega|Du|&\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}\int_\Omega|Du_n|
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(iii) To each $u_n$, we can associate from the above a unique
$P_n\in H^1(\Omega)$ such that $-\Delta P_n=f-u_n$ and
$\int_\Omega P_n(x)\,dx=0$. Prove, still using
Poincar\'e--Wirtinger's inequality, that $(P_n)_n$ is uniformly
bounded in $H^1(\Omega)$. Deduce that there exists a subsequence
of $(P_n)_n$, still denoted by $(P_n)_n$, and $P\in H^1(\Omega)$
such that $P_n\to P$ in $L^2(\Omega)$. Justify that
$\int_\Omega P(x)\,dx=0$ and that $f-u=-\Delta P$.
\end{statement}

\solution
Let $\lambda>0$. Set $\bar f=|\Omega|^{-1}\int_\Omega f$ and
$A=(-\Delta_N)^{-1}$ on mean-zero functions. Then
$E(u)=\TV(u)+\lambda\norm{\nabla A(f-u)}_2^2$.

\partlabel{a(i)}
The kernel $V_0$ of the continuous map $P\mapsto\int_\Omega P$
is closed. Poincar\'e--Wirtinger gives
\begin{align*}
 \norm{\nabla P}_2&\leq\norm P_{H^1}
       \leq\sqrt{1+C^2}\norm{\nabla P}_2
\end{align*}
Thus $\ip PQ_{V_0}=\int_\Omega\nabla P\cdot\nabla Q$ induces a complete norm.

\partlabel{a(ii)}
For $P,Q\in V_0$, set
\begin{align*}
 a(P,Q)&=\int_\Omega\nabla P\cdot\nabla Q\\
 \ell(Q)&=\int_\Omega vQ
\end{align*}
Then
\begin{align*}
 a(P,P)&=\norm P_{V_0}^2\\
 |\ell(Q)|&\leq C\norm v_2\norm Q_{V_0}
\end{align*}
Lax--Milgram gives a unique $P\in V_0$ with
\begin{align*}
 \int_\Omega\nabla P\cdot\nabla Q&=\int_\Omega vQ
\end{align*}
for every $Q\in H^1(\Omega)$, since $\int_\Omega v=0$.
This is the weak Neumann equation. Taking $Q=P$,
\begin{align*}
 \norm{\nabla P}_2&\leq C\norm v_2
\end{align*}

\partlabel{b}
Let $P=A(f-u)$ with $\int_\Omega u=\int_\Omega f$.
For mean-zero $h$,
\begin{align*}
 D\!\left(\lambda\norm{\nabla P}_2^2\right)[h]
 &=-2\lambda\int_\Omega\nabla P\cdot\nabla(Ah)\\
 &=-2\lambda\int_\Omega Ph
\end{align*}
For smooth $u$, set $z=\nabla u/|\nabla u|$ where $|\nabla u|>0$.
With $z\cdot n=0$,
\begin{align*}
 DE(u)[h]&=\int_\Omega z\cdot\nabla h-2\lambda\int_\Omega Ph\\
 &=\int_\Omega(-\diver z-2\lambda P)h
\end{align*}
At a constrained critical point, $q=-\diver z-2\lambda P$ is
constant and has zero mean. Thus
\begin{align*}
 E_u=q&=-\diver\left(\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\right)-2\lambda P=0\\
 -\Delta P&=f-u\\
 \partial_nP&=0
\end{align*}
For nonsmooth $u$, use $2\lambda P\in\partial\TV(u)$.

\partlabel{c}
Impose $\partial_nq=0$ and $\int_\Omega u_0=\int_\Omega f$.
For smooth solutions,
\begin{align*}
 u_t&=\Delta q\\
 &=-\Delta\diver\left(\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\right)
      +2\lambda(f-u)\\
 \frac{d}{dt}\int_\Omega u\,dx
 &=\int_\Omega\Delta q\,dx\\
 &=\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_nq\,dS=0\\
 \int_\Omega(f-u(t))\,dx&=0
\end{align*}
\partlabel{d}
\begin{align*}
 \frac{d}{dt}E(u(t))
 &=\int_\Omega q\,u_t\,dx\\
 &=\int_\Omega q\Delta q\,dx\\
 &=-\int_\Omega|\nabla q|^2\,dx\leq0
\end{align*}

\partlabel{e(i)}
Using $u=\bar f$ and (a),
\begin{align*}
 0\leq\inf E&\leq E(\bar f)\\
 &=\lambda\norm{\nabla A(f-\bar f)}_2^2\\
 &\leq\lambda C^2\norm{f-\bar f}_2^2<\infty
\end{align*}

\partlabel{e(ii)}
Let $E(u_n)\leq M$. Since $\bar u_n=\bar f$, Poincar\'e--Wirtinger gives
\begin{align*}
 \TV(u_n)&\leq M\\
 \norm{u_n-\bar f}_1&\leq C\TV(u_n)\\
 \norm{u_n}_1&\leq CM+|\Omega||\bar f|\\
 &\leq CM+|\Omega|^{1/2}\norm f_2
\end{align*}
By $BV$ compactness and lower semicontinuity, along a subsequence,
\begin{align*}
 \norm{u_n-u}_1&\longrightarrow0\\
 \int_\Omega u\,dx&=\int_\Omega f\,dx\\
 \TV(u)&\leq\liminf_n\TV(u_n)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{e(iii)}
For $P_n=A(f-u_n)$,
\begin{align*}
 \norm{\nabla P_n}_2^2&\leq M/\lambda\\
 \norm{P_n}_{H^1}&\leq\sqrt{1+C^2}\norm{\nabla P_n}_2
\end{align*}
By Rellich compactness, $P_n\rightharpoonup P$ in $H^1(\Omega)$
along a subsequence, with
\begin{align*}
 \norm{P_n-P}_2&\longrightarrow0\\
 \int_\Omega P\,dx&=\lim_n\int_\Omega P_n\,dx=0
\end{align*}
Since $BV(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^2(\Omega)$ and $u_n\to u$ in
$L^1$, we have $u_n\rightharpoonup u$ in $L^2$.
For $\psi\in H^1(\Omega)$,
\begin{align*}
 \int_\Omega\nabla P\cdot\nabla\psi
 &=\lim_n\int_\Omega\nabla P_n\cdot\nabla\psi\\
 &=\lim_n\int_\Omega(f-u_n)\psi\\
 &=\int_\Omega(f-u)\psi
\end{align*}
Thus $P=A(f-u)$. Lower semicontinuity gives
\begin{align*}
 E(u)&=\TV(u)+\lambda\norm{\nabla P}_2^2\\
 &\leq\liminf_n E(u_n)=\inf E
 \tag*{$\square$}
\end{align*}

\clearpage
\exercise{5.3}
\begin{statement}
In this exercise, related with examples given in this chapter, and
following Chan, Esedo\u{g}lu and Nikolova [88], we illustrate the fact
that continuous curve evolution-based gradient descent algorithms can
get stuck in a local minimizer according to the chosen initial condition.
The assumptions are given next. The observed image $f$ is binary, that is,
$f(x)\in\{0,1\}$ for all $x\in\R^N$ and is assumed to be a corrupted
version of another binary image $u:\R^N\to\{0,1\}$ to be recovered.
$f$ is thus expressed as $f(x)=\chi_\Omega(x)$ with $\Omega$ an arbitrary
bounded measurable subset of $\R^N$ and we search for $u$ of the form
$u(x)=\chi_\Sigma(x)$ with $\Sigma$ a subset of $\R^N$. We consider the
following nonconvex optimization problem:
\begin{align*}
 \min_{\substack{\Sigma\subset\R^N\\u(x)=\chi_\Sigma(x)}}
 \int_{\R^N}|Du|+\lambda\int_{\R^N}
       (u(x)-\chi_\Omega(x))^2\,dx
 \tag{5.15}
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(a) Show that (5.15) can be rephrased as
\begin{align*}
 \min_{\substack{\Sigma\subset\R^N\\u(x)=\chi_\Sigma(x)}}
 \operatorname{Per}(\Sigma)+\lambda|\Sigma\mathbin\triangle\Omega|
\end{align*}
where $\operatorname{Per}(\cdot)$ denotes the perimeter of the set,
$|\cdot|$ is the $N$-dimensional Lebesgue measure and
$S_1\mathbin\triangle S_2$ denotes the symmetric difference between
the two sets $S_1$ and $S_2$.
\par\smallskip
(b) A usual technique of approximation consists in expressing the problem
in the level set framework. Let us thus denote by $\Phi:\R^N\to\R$ a
Lipschitz continuous function such that
$\Sigma=\{x\in\R^N\mid\Phi(x)>0\}$ and
$\partial\Sigma=\{x\in\R^N\mid\Phi(x)=0\}$.
\par\smallskip
(i) Show that (5.15) can be reformulated by means of the functional
\begin{align*}
 E(\Phi,\lambda)
 &=\int_{\R^N}|DH(\Phi)|
   +\lambda\int_{\R^N}(H(\Phi(x))-\chi_\Omega(x))^2\,dx
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
(ii) By replacing the one-dimensional Heaviside function $H$ by a smooth
approximation $H_\eps$, give the Euler--Lagrange equation associated with
functional $E$ and derive the gradient flow. Show that the energy
decreases in time.
\par\smallskip
(iii) Consider the two-dimensional case $f=\chi_{B_R(0)}$ with $B_R(0)$
the ball of radius $R$, centered at the origin. As initial condition, we
take $\Phi$ such that $\Sigma=B_R(0)$.
\par\smallskip
a Justify that the evolution equation maintains radial symmetry of
$\Phi$. It means that at any given time $t\geq0$, the candidate for
minimization represented through $\Phi$ is of the form
$\{x\in\R^2\mid\Phi(x)>0\}=B_r(0)$ with $r\geq0$ a radius.
As a consequence, $u(x)=\chi_{B_r(0)}(x)$.
\par\smallskip
b Show that the energy involved in minimization problem (5.15) can be
rewritten under the form
\begin{align*}
 J(r)&=2\pi r+\lambda\pi|R^2-r^2|
\end{align*}
\par\smallskip
c Show that if $R<\frac2\lambda$, the minimum of $J$ is at $r=0$.
In practice, it means that the denoising model removes disks of radius
smaller than the value $\frac2R$.
\par\smallskip
d Show that if $R>\frac1\lambda$, $J$ has a local maximum at
$r=\frac1\lambda$.
\par\smallskip
e Conclude that if the initial condition is chosen to be $u=f$ with
$f=\chi_{B_R(0)}$ with $R\in]\frac1\lambda,\frac2\lambda[$, the curve
evolution-based gradient descent algorithm remains stuck in the local
minimizer, while the global minimizer is $u=0$.
\end{statement}

\solution
\partlabel{a}
For $u=\chi_\Sigma$,
\begin{align*}
 |D\chi_\Sigma|(\R^N)&=\operatorname{Per}(\Sigma)\\
 (\chi_\Sigma-\chi_\Omega)^2
 &=\chi_{\Sigma\mathbin\triangle\Omega}
\end{align*}

\partlabel{b(i)}
For $H=\chi_{(0,\infty)}$, substitute $H(\Phi)=\chi_\Sigma$ into (5.15).

\partlabel{b(ii)}
Let $H_\eps$ be nondecreasing with compactly supported derivative,
$\delta_\eps=H_\eps'$, and $\kappa=\diver(\nabla\Phi/|\nabla\Phi|)$. Then
\begin{align*}
 E_\eps(\Phi)
 &=\int_{\R^N}\delta_\eps(\Phi)|\nabla\Phi|\,dx
   +\lambda\int_{\R^N}(H_\eps(\Phi)-f)^2\,dx
\end{align*}
Assume $|\nabla\Phi|>0$ on the support of $\delta_\eps(\Phi)$ and
vanishing boundary terms. For a variation $h$,
\begin{align*}
 DE_\eps(\Phi)[h]
 &=\int\delta_\eps'(\Phi)|\nabla\Phi|h
   +\int\delta_\eps(\Phi)
       \frac{\nabla\Phi}{|\nabla\Phi|}\cdot\nabla h\\
 &\quad+2\lambda\int(H_\eps(\Phi)-f)\delta_\eps(\Phi)h\\
 &=\int\delta_\eps(\Phi)
       \bigl[-\kappa+2\lambda(H_\eps(\Phi)-f)\bigr]h
\end{align*}
Hence
\begin{align*}
 \delta_\eps(\Phi)
   \bigl[-\kappa+2\lambda(H_\eps(\Phi)-f)\bigr]&=0\\
 \Phi_t&=\delta_\eps(\Phi)
   \bigl[\kappa-2\lambda(H_\eps(\Phi)-f)\bigr]
\end{align*}
Along a smooth solution,
\begin{align*}
 \frac{d}{dt}E_\eps(\Phi(t))
 &=DE_\eps(\Phi)[\Phi_t]\\
 &=-\int_{\R^N}|\Phi_t|^2\,dx\leq0
\end{align*}

\partlabel{b(iii)a}
Choose $\Phi(x,0)=R-|x|$. For a unique evolution, rotation invariance
gives $\Phi(Qx,t)=\Phi(x,t)$ for every rotation $Q$.
The single circular interface remains a circle until extinction,
so $\Sigma(t)=B_{r(t)}(0)$. This uses radial initial data and a
single-interface evolution; the initial positive set alone is insufficient.

\partlabel{b(iii)b}
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{Per}(B_r)&=2\pi r\\
 |B_r\mathbin\triangle B_R|&=\pi|r^2-R^2|
\end{align*}

\partlabel{b(iii)c}
On $[0,R]$,
\begin{align*}
 J(r)&=\lambda\pi R^2+2\pi r-\lambda\pi r^2\\
 J''(r)&=-2\lambda\pi<0
\end{align*}
Its minimum is at an endpoint. On $[R,\infty)$,
\begin{align*}
 J'(r)&=2\pi+2\lambda\pi r>0
\end{align*}
If $R<2/\lambda$,
\begin{align*}
 J(0)&=\lambda\pi R^2<2\pi R=J(R)
\end{align*}
Hence $r=0$ uniquely minimizes $J$. The printed $2/R$ should be
$2/\lambda$.

For any set $\Sigma$ of finite measure and perimeter, set
$\rho=\sqrt{|\Sigma|/\pi}$. By the isoperimetric inequality,
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{Per}(\Sigma)&\geq2\pi\rho\\
 |\Sigma\mathbin\triangle B_R|
 &\geq\bigl||\Sigma|-|B_R|\bigr|\\
 &=\pi|\rho^2-R^2|\\
 \operatorname{Per}(\Sigma)+\lambda|\Sigma\mathbin\triangle B_R|
 &\geq J(\rho)\geq J(0)
\end{align*}

\partlabel{b(iii)d}
If $R>1/\lambda$, then $1/\lambda\in(0,R)$,
$J'(1/\lambda)=0$, and $J''(1/\lambda)=-2\lambda\pi<0$.

\partlabel{b(iii)e}
For $1/\lambda<R<2/\lambda$,
\begin{align*}
 J'_-(R)&=2\pi(1-\lambda R)<0\\
 J'_+(R)&=2\pi(1+\lambda R)>0
\end{align*}
Thus $r=R$ is a strict local minimum, so continuous descent stays there,
although $J(0)<J(R)$. This applies to $J$; smoothing $H$ may shift the
stationary circle.
\finish

\end{document}
